- **`predict.py`** (wave C1, review finding C1) — pure stdlib. `@dataclass(frozen=True) Prediction(box, confidence)`. `predict(track, now) -> Prediction` — constant-velocity extrapolation of `track.box` from `track.last_seen` to `now` (clamped by `_MAX_EXTRAPOLATION_SECONDS`). **No `transform` argument** (wave C2 removed one that briefly existed here — see "Ego-motion compensation" below): `TrackBook.warp()` now carries `track.box`/`velocity_*` through every frame of camera motion BEFORE this function ever runs, so there is nothing left for `predict()` itself to warp; a `transform` parameter here would be a second mechanism doing the same job the book already did, which is exactly the undercorrection bug wave C2 fixed. `confidence` decays from `track.last_confirmed`, not `last_seen` (`_CONFIDENCE_DECAY_SECONDS`) — feeds a future association gate (`assign.py`, wave C3), unconsumed elsewhere. This is THE fix for "a lost tracker freezes the box instead of predicting it": every place `session.py` used to hold `held.box` unchanged now calls `predict()` instead, including the FOLLOW verify pass's own re-anchor test (matching this frame's detections against where the target is now believed to be, not where it was several coasted frames ago).
- **`registry.py`** — `BUILTIN_ASSOCIATORS`/`BUILTIN_FOLLOWERS`/`BUILTIN_COMPENSATORS`/`BUILTIN_APPEARANCES: dict[str, factory]` (lazy imports inside the factories; `BUILTIN_ASSOCIATORS = {"bytetrack": ..., "cost": ...}` — wave C3 adds `"cost"`; `BUILTIN_COMPENSATORS = {"flow": ..., "pose": ...}`, wave C2's third roster; `BUILTIN_APPEARANCES = {"histogram": ...}`, wave C3's fourth). `MOTION_ENGINE_FLOW = "flow"` / `MOTION_ENGINE_POSE = "pose"` — engine-id constants `session.py` imports for its pose→flow fallback policy, living here (not in `engines/flow_gmc.py`, which is `cv2` at module scope) so that policy can be checked without threatening "importable without the `cv` extra". The `_cost` factory (wave C3) builds `assign.CostAssociator` with NEUTRAL `AssignWeights()`/`AssignGates()` — unlike every other associator/follower factory it is never handed the stream's resolved cost configuration (`TrackerRegistry`'s own construction contract has no place for it, uniform `max_age_frames`-only across the associator roster); `session.py`'s `_run_cost_associate` calls `CostAssociator.retune(weights=, gates=)` once per frame instead, cheap enough at this scale to just always do. `TrackerRegistry(*, associators, followers, compensators, appearances, default_associate_id, default_follow_id, default_motion_id, default_appearance_id)`: `.probe() -> dict[str, list[str]]` (constructs each engine once across all FOUR rosters, drops what raises, logs the surviving roster **once at INFO** including all four defaults; idempotent, never raises), `.roster()` (a compensator's role tag is `"MOTION_COMPENSATOR"`, an extractor's is `"APPEARANCE_EXTRACTOR"` — neither is a `TrackingMode`, since neither is tied to one), `.associator(engine_id, *, max_age_frames)` / `.follower(...)` / `.compensator(engine_id)` / `.appearance(engine_id)` (the last two take no `max_age_frames` — meaningless for a motion/appearance engine) → `(serving_engine_id, engine) | None` — **a NEW instance per call**, unknown id logged once and served the default. `build_default_registry(settings, *, probe=True) -> TrackerRegistry` — never `None` (an empty roster is a usable object that degrades every stream, and returning `None` would move the same branch into every caller); now wires all seven built-in engines (previously five). **Wave V1 makes every roster level-aware**: `associator`/`follower`/`compensator`/`appearance` each gain an optional `level: int | None = None` — `None` (the default, so every pre-V1 call site and every duck-typed test double elsewhere in the suite keeps working unchanged) means no ceiling. When given, `_create` filters the roster against a per-role `_{ASSOCIATOR,FOLLOWER,COMPENSATOR,APPEARANCE}_MIN_LEVEL: dict[str, int]` **before** the `engine_id`/default/fallback ladder or any factory ever runs — the mechanism that makes L1 provably `cv2`/`numpy`-free (invariant P8): a level-excluded engine's factory is never called, not merely discarded after construction. Per decision E11 (measured, §5.1): `cost` (pure stdlib) is selectable from L1; `bytetrack` (drags `numpy` in, needs `ultralytics` to construct) only from L3, where `numpy` is already resident for the detector so its own +17 MiB is already paid. `pose` (pure trigonometry) is selectable from L1; `lk`/`ncc`/`flow`/`histogram` (all need `cv2`) only from L2. The filter is a CONTRACT independent of what a more capable host happens to be able to build (invariant P9) — a stream explicitly capped to L1 on a workstation that has `cv2` fully installed still never sees it.
- **`engines/histogram.py`** (NEW, wave C3) — `ENGINE_ID = "histogram"`; `HistogramAppearanceExtractor()` (an `AppearanceExtractor`) and `create()`. Needs `cv2` — the third named exception to `engines/`'s pure-stdlib-outside-these rule (P3), alongside `bytetrack.py`/`lk.py`/`flow_gmc.py`. Stateless across frames (unlike every other engine here) — a colour histogram needs nothing but the current frame and box, so per-stream construction exists only to match the registry's convention, not because there is state to hold. `describe(frame, boxes)`: `cv2.cvtColor(frame, COLOR_BGR2HSV)` once per call, then per box: clip to the frame, `None` if the clipped region is empty or under `_MIN_DESCRIBABLE_PIXELS`; else `cv2.calcHist` a coarse joint H×S histogram (`_HUE_BINS=16`, `_SATURATION_BINS=8`), L1-normalized, wrapped as `Descriptor(ENGINE_ID, values, METRIC_HELLINGER)`. Value (brightness) is deliberately dropped — the channel most sensitive to exposure/shadow a moving camera or subject produces, and dropping it is cheaper than weighting it down. `reset()` is a no-op — nothing to drop.
- **`session.py`** — `@dataclass(frozen=True) TrackedBox(label, confidence, box, track, identity_confidence=0.0, dormant_millis=0)` (`track is None` is the one spelling of untracked; the last two fields, wave C4, are per-FRAME facts about a recovery EVENT, not per-track state — `0.0`/`0` on every frame that is not the exact one a recovery happened on, set only by `_run_cost_associate`). `@dataclass(frozen=True) FrameOutcome(boxes, inference_millis, detector_ran, detector_reason, tracker_millis, engine_id, locked_track_id, motion_millis, motion_engine_id, ..., reupdate_millis=0, reupdated_tracks=0)` (`boxes is None` → echo this frame; the motion pair, wave C2, are `0`/`""` on OFF always and on ASSOCIATE unless `cost` is the resolved engine — see below; `reupdate_millis`/`reupdated_tracks`, wave V3, are `0` on any frame that never calls `TrackBook.apply()` at all — `TrackBook.reset_reupdate_stats()`'s own guarantee, not merely a usual outcome — and read straight off `self._book.last_reupdate_millis`/`.last_reupdated_tracks` AFTER the mode dispatch). `StreamTrackingSession(*, settings, registry_provider)`: `.active`, `.params`, `.tracks`, `.applied_wire_config` (opaque, compared by the servicer), `.apply_config(TrackingRequest, wire_token)` (**on change only**), `.process(*, now_millis, detect, frame, pose=CameraPose()) -> FrameOutcome`. `detect` and `frame` are callables the servicer supplies — which is exactly why this file **holds no reference to an `InferenceGate` and structurally cannot acquire one**; `pose` (wave C2) is a plain value, not a callable — cheap enough to pass unconditionally. Wave C1: `_coast` calls `predict.predict()` wherever it used to freeze the box, and additionally raises trigger (b) (`_tracker_failed`) on a tracker-only frame whose `TrackerUpdate.confidence` falls below `params.min_tracker_confidence` — same "only on a tracker-only frame" restriction as the hard-failure case, so it cannot collapse the duty cycle either. `_reset_engine`/`_release_engine` call `TrackBook.bump_epoch()` instead of `.forget_keys()` (review findings D1/D2) — `_reset_state` (tracking going OFF) is the one path that still calls `.forget_keys()` for a genuine wipe. **Wave C2** — `process()` calls `_estimate_motion(pose, frame, now)` exactly ONCE per frame, before the mode branches, but ONLY when `self._params.mode == MODE_FOLLOW`; the resulting `Transform` is then handed to `self._book.warp(transform)` — called EVERY frame regardless of mode (a guaranteed no-op outside FOLLOW, since `transform` is `IDENTITY` there) — BEFORE the mode dispatch, so `_run_associate`/`_follow_verify`/`_follow_predict` all see already-warped track state. `_follow_verify`/`_follow_predict`/`_coast`/`_select_target`/`_box_of_track` take **no `transform` parameter** — see `predict.py`'s and `track.py`'s own entries above for why the warp lives in the book, not threaded through these calls (TRACKING-V2-PLAN wave C2's own fix for an undercorrection found in review — full account in "Ego-motion compensation" below). `_resolve_motion_compensator(pose)` mirrors `_resolve_engine`'s build-once shape, plus a policy `_resolve_engine` doesn't need: a requested `"pose"` engine that is CONSTRUCTIBLE but reports `available(pose) is False` (no FOV on the wire — the documented normal state today) falls back to `"flow"`, logged once per engine id (`_degraded_motion_ids`); `"off"` (`_MOTION_ENGINE_OFF`, a local constant — not a `params.py` sentinel, see that module's own note) skips the registry entirely; nothing constructible → no compensation, never an exception. `apply_config` releases the compensator (`_release_motion_compensator`) independently of the SOT engine — only when `motion_engine_id` itself changes, or on a genuine stop (`_reset_state`) — an operator switching `lk` ↔ `ncc` does not reset the camera-motion reference, and vice versa.
  **Wave C3 — the keystone.** `process()`'s compensation gate is no longer just `MODE_FOLLOW`: it also fires for `MODE_ASSOCIATE` when `self._engine_id == CostAssociator.engine_id` (`"cost"`) — checked AFTER `_resolve_engine()` has already run, so it reads the ACTUAL serving engine, not the requested one. `bytetrack` still never compensates (its association state lives inside a third-party Kalman filter with no seam to warp — unchanged reasoning from C2), which is what keeps the DEFAULT ASSOCIATE path's cost byte-for-byte unaffected by this wave (measured — see "Association inversion" below) even though the gate condition itself changed. `_run_associate` dispatches to `_run_cost_associate` on the same check; the old `engine.associate(detections, now)` path is otherwise completely untouched. `_run_cost_associate(engine, detections, now, frame)`: builds one `assign.Candidate` per `self._book.tracks` (`key=track.key` — the RAW engine-token TrackBook already indexes by, so a matched pair's `Observation.key` round-trips onto the SAME track; `box=predict(track, now).box` — already warped this frame by `TrackBook.warp()`, so `predict()` needs nothing extra; `descriptor=track.descriptor`; `confirmed=track.state != STATE_TENTATIVE`) and one `assign.Target` per detection (`descriptor=` from `_describe`, see below); resolves `weights = params.cost_weights` and zeroes `weights.appearance` when NO appearance extractor resolved this stream (`extractor is None`, checked once via `_resolve_appearance_extractor()`, not per-frame descriptor presence — a frame with no describable boxes must not read as "appearance is off for this stream", since `assign.py`'s own missing-descriptor handling already covers that case); calls `engine.retune(weights=, gates=params.cost_gates)` then `.assign(candidates, targets)`. Matched pairs become `Observation(key=candidate.key, box=target.box, ...)`; unmatched targets become NEW `Observation`s keyed by a fresh `object()` sentinel (`cost` allocates no identity of its own, unlike `bytetrack`'s key counter — a permanently-unique token is all `TrackBook._namespaced` needs). After `TrackBook.apply()` books everything, `track.observe_descriptor(track, target.descriptor)` runs for every booked track — EMA'ing the appearance signature from DETECTOR evidence only, same evidence-vs-extrapolation rule velocity already follows. **Unmatched candidates are deliberately NOT filtered by state** — a `STATE_LOST` track still sitting in `TrackBook` (not yet `_expire()`d, i.e. within `max_age_frames × 2`) is still offered as a `Candidate`; this is not a memory tier (wave C4's `ObjectMemory` is a separate, four-gated, TTL-bounded gallery for identities that have left `TrackBook` ENTIRELY), it mirrors `bytetrack`'s own internal `track_buffer` re-matching a lost id within its own retention window, just done through `TrackBook`'s existing bookkeeping instead of a third-party Kalman filter's. `_describe(extractor, frame, boxes)`: `None` for every box when `extractor is None`; else `extractor.describe(frame(), boxes)`, degrading (logged once per engine id, engine dropped and re-resolved next frame) rather than raising on a bad extractor — same posture `_estimate_motion` already takes. `_resolve_appearance_extractor()` mirrors `_resolve_motion_compensator`'s build-once shape but simpler: no live-signal check like `CameraPose` (constructibility IS availability for an extractor), so no fallback ladder — `"off"` (`_APPEARANCE_ENGINE_OFF`) or the one thing asked for. Only ever reached from `_run_cost_associate` — `bytetrack` never resolves it and never decodes a frame for appearance purposes. `apply_config` releases the extractor (`_release_appearance_extractor`) independently, only when `appearance_engine_id` itself changes or on a genuine stop, same shape as the motion compensator's release.

  **Wave C4 — the object memory, wired in.** Unlike motion/appearance, `_resolve_memory()` is called UNCONDITIONALLY once near the top of `process()` (right after `_resolve_engine()`), for every ACTIVE mode/engine, not only when `cost` is serving — `TrackBook._retire` has to be able to remember a LOST track regardless of which associator produced it (a `bytetrack`-associate or FOLLOW track that later expires is remembered too, harmless unless an operator later switches that stream to `cost`, in which case it becomes genuinely recoverable — proven end to end by `test_a_track_expiring_under_one_associator_is_recovered_after_switching_to_cost`). Mirrors `_resolve_engine`'s build-once shape (an `active`-gated early return, not `_resolve_appearance_extractor`'s unconditional one, since THIS resolver has to run even when nothing has asked for `cost` yet): `TrackingParams.memory_params.ttl_millis <= 0` (deployment- or request-disabled) leaves `self._memory` (and `self._book`'s own copy, via `set_memory`) at `None` — the genuine no-op P5 requires, no gallery object built or consulted. Only `_run_cost_associate` ever QUERIES it back (the descriptor pipeline `ObjectMemory.match()` can use only exists there): for each of `assignment.unmatched_targets`, `_attempt_recovery(target, now)` calls `self._memory.match(...)` (read-only) then `.claim(...)` (commits) — `claim()` returning `None` means a same-frame race (two unmatched targets both scored above threshold against the SAME dormant entry; whichever's turn comes first in `assignment.unmatched_targets` wins it, everyone else falls back to a fresh id, never a forced double-claim onto two different objects — this is precisely the crowd risk `MODULE.md`'s clutter finding below is about). A recovery is booked through `TrackBook.apply(..., recoveries={key: RecoveredIdentity(...)})` in the SAME call that books everything else — `key` is the same fresh `object()` sentinel already minted for the `Observation`, so `recoveries` and `observations` stay positionally independent but keyed identically. `identity_confidence`/`dormant_millis` (`Recovery.confidence`/`.dormant_millis`) are threaded onto the response ONLY for the det_index that was actually recovered this frame (`recovery_by_index`), via new keyword args on `_box_for`. `apply_config` treats a changed `memory_params` the same way the cost weights do NOT need to be treated (they have no wire override to react to) but `motion_engine_id`/`appearance_engine_id` DO: `self._memory.retune(new_params)` in place if a gallery already exists and stays enabled, else `_release_memory()` (safe whether or not anything was ever built) so the next active frame decides fresh — this is what lets a request TTL crossing the enabled/disabled line take effect without a stream restart. `_reset_state()` (tracking OFF) also calls `_release_memory()`, same as the other two engines.

  **Wave V3 — two additions, both small.** `process()` calls `self._book.reset_reupdate_stats()` once, BEFORE the mode dispatch (so a frame that never calls `apply()` still reports `0`/`0`, not a stale prior frame's numbers — see the `TrackBook` entry above); `FrameOutcome`'s `reupdate_millis`/`reupdated_tracks` are read from `self._book.last_reupdate_millis`/`.last_reupdated_tracks` AFTER it. Second, `_select_target` (FOLLOW's re-anchor test, called from `_follow_verify`) gains a fallback: when the primary `lock_module.select_target` call (predicted-box IoU) returns no match and a held box exists, it tries once more against the locked track's LAST REAL observation (`self._followed.history.latest()`), warped into the current frame by `self._followed.history_transform` and matched via `lock_module.best_iou_match` at the SAME `redetect_iou_threshold` — gated by `0.0 < gap_millis <= self._params.reupdate_max_gap_millis`, the SAME ceiling `reupdate()` itself is bounded by. Full design, why this exists (ORU is unreachable in FOLLOW without it), and its three safety properties are in "Observation-Centric Re-Update" below.

### `cv_service/grpc/servicers.py`
The **sole place** that touches generated `cv_pb2` message types — every other cv-service module works with plain Python values/dataclasses. Owns the `sys.path`/`cv_service/gen` bootstrap (`cv_service/grpc/server.py` re-imports `cv_pb2`/`cv_pb2_grpc` from here rather than duplicating it).
- `class InferenceServicer(cv_pb2_grpc.InferenceServicer)` — `__init__(detector: YoloDetector | None = None, *, inference_gate: InferenceGate | None = None, registry: ModelRegistry | None = None)`. Two mutually-exclusive ways to give it a model:
  - **`detector=`** (explicit injection — every pre-registry test still uses this): exactly one detector serves every frame regardless of `model_id`; an unrecognized `model_id` logs once (`_warn_once_on_unknown_model`, unchanged) and serves this same detector anyway. No registry, no composite mode.
  - **omitted** (the default/production path): builds a `ModelRegistry` via the module-local `_build_default_registry()` (a thin wrapper resolving `Settings.from_env()` and lazily importing `cv_service.inference.registry.build_default_registry`) — or takes `registry=` directly, for tests — `request.model_id` (comma-separated for composite) is routed through `registry.resolve()` + `registry.detect_composite()` in `_detect_via_registry()`.
  `inference_gate` defaults to `cv_service.inference.concurrency.process_gate()` (shared process-wide) — tests inject a smaller/differently-sized one; production (`serve()`) always builds and passes one explicitly from `Settings`. `DetectStream(request_iterator, context)`: runs real inference per frame when a model is loaded (either path), mapping `Detection`s to `cv_pb2.Detection`/`BoundingBox`; "no model at all" and any per-frame exception degrade to the original Phase 0 echo response — a bad frame or missing model never kills the bidi stream. **When a model is loaded, frame receipt and inference run concurrently within the stream, and every `detect()` call is gated across streams** — see "V-d: parallel per-stream inference" below.
  Two more constructor kwargs land with T1: **`settings=`** (a resolved `Settings`; falls back to `Settings.from_env()`, the same per-construction-refresh idiom `YoloDetector` uses) supplies the `CV_TRACK_*` defaults `params.resolve()` falls back to, and **`tracker_registry=`** injects the shared `TrackerRegistry`. Omitting `tracker_registry` builds one **lazily**, on the first frame that actually requests an active tracking mode, so neither the OFF path nor a test that never tracks pays for constructing and probing engines; `serve()` passes an already-probed one instead, which is what makes the roster get logged at *startup* rather than on first use.
  `DetectStream` creates one **`StreamTrackingSession`** per call, in the same place and for the same reason `_StreamReader` is created (state that belongs to one bidi call, not to the servicer every stream shares), and threads it into `_handle_request(request, session)`.
- Tracking wire translation lives here and nowhere else (module-level, T1): `_tracking_request_from_wire` / `_lock_request_from_wire` (`cv_pb2.TrackingConfig`/`TargetLock` → the plain `TrackingRequest`/`LockRequest`; wave C2 adds `motion_engine_id` to the former, wave C3 adds `appearance_engine_id`, wave C4 adds `memory_ttl_millis` — field 10, frozen at C0, finally read here; wave V3 adds `reupdate_max_gap_millis` — field 14, same "read it straight, `params.resolve()` interprets the sentinel" shape as every field above it), `_tracking_mode_name` (an unknown mode number from a *newer* client → `UNSPECIFIED` → OFF, never a raise), `_camera_pose_from_wire` (NEW, wave C2 — `cv_pb2.CameraPose` → the plain `CameraPose` from `engines/base.py`; no `HasField` check needed, since every field read is a scalar whose proto3 zero already means what the plain type's own default means), `_tracked_detection` (a `TrackedBox` → `cv_pb2.Detection`; an untracked box sets **no** track field at all; wave C4 additionally copies `box.identity_confidence`/`.dormant_millis` straight through to `Detection` fields 10/11 UNCONDITIONALLY — not gated on `track is not None`, since a `TrackedBox` not involved in a recovery already carries `0.0`/`0`, and a proto3 scalar assignment of the zero value is a wire no-op either way; wave V3 adds `detection.reupdated = track.reupdated` — field 14, read straight off `Track` inside the `track is not None` branch, same as `velocity_x`/`state`/`age_frames`), `_tracked_response` (a `FrameOutcome` → `DetectionResponse`, including `detector_ran`/`detector_reason`/`tracker_millis`/`tracker_engine_id`/`locked_track_id`/`motion_millis`/`motion_engine_id`/`detector_roi` (wave C5c, field 13) — no wire field exists for appearance evidence/cost/memory-gallery-size, so none of those are on the response; wave V3 adds `reupdate_millis`/`reupdated_tracks` — fields 22/23, straight off `FrameOutcome`, `0`/`0` when ORU never ran this frame), and `_frame_loader` (a **memoized, lazy** `decode_frame` closure — lazy because `ASSOCIATE`/`bytetrack` never needs pixels and importing `detector.py` at module scope would break echo mode without the `cv` extra; memoized because a FOLLOW verify frame touches the pixels twice, and also because `_estimate_motion` (wave C2), `_describe` (wave C3, when `cost` is active) and, since wave C5c, `_run_roi_detector`'s own crop each reuse the SAME decoded frame rather than decoding again). `_handle_request` builds ONE `loader = _frame_loader(request)` and passes it as BOTH `frame=loader` and (wave C5c) folded into `detect=lambda roi=None: self._run_detector(request, roi, loader)` — the single construction site that makes "no second decode" a structural guarantee rather than a convention two separate call sites have to remember. `_handle_request` also passes `pose=_camera_pose_from_wire(request.camera_pose)` into `session.process(...)` — only on the active-tracking branch, so an `OFF` frame never touches `camera_pose` at all (P1 stays exact).
- `InferenceServicer._run_detector(request, roi=None, frame_loader=None)` is the **one place `InferenceGate` is acquired** — both model paths (explicit `detector=` and registry-routed) fold into it precisely so the tracking session can spend a detector pass through a single callable without knowing which one the servicer was built with, and so the gate acquisition stays a single greppable location. `roi=None` (every call before wave C5c, and the overwhelming majority after it) takes the untouched full-frame branch, byte-for-byte (P1); `roi=<Box>` (only ever from `session.py`'s `_roi_rescue`, at most once per frame) delegates to NEW `_run_roi_detector(request, roi, frame_loader)` — crops the ALREADY-decoded frame (`frame_loader()`, never a second `decode_frame`), repackages the crop as raw `IMAGE_ENCODING_BGR24` bytes (no JPEG round-trip) so it feeds the SAME `width=`/`height=`/`encoding=`/`data=` shape every detector call in this file already uses, runs it through the SAME registry-or-explicit-detector branch (`_detect_via_registry` gained optional `width=`/`height=`/`encoding=`/`data=` overrides for exactly this, defaulting to `request`'s own fields so every pre-C5c call site is unaffected), and maps every returned detection back to full-frame normalized coordinates (module-level `_map_roi_detection`, THE coordinate round-trip: `full = roi.origin + crop_relative * roi.extent`, per axis). NEW module-level `_crop_for_roi(image, roi) -> (cropped, width, height) | None` does the normalized→pixel rounding and refuses a crop under `_ROI_MIN_PIXELS` on a side — a degenerate ROI, a missing `frame_loader`, or a frame-decode failure each degrade to `([], 0)` (P5: costs this ONE pass, never raises, never the stream, logged at most once per servicer instance via `_warn_roi_degraded`) rather than touching the detector or the gate at all. `_sync_tracking(session, request)` folds in this frame's restated `TrackingConfig` (one protobuf `==` per frame; `resolve()` and any engine rebuild only on a real change) and reports whether tracking is active.
- `class _StreamReader` — package-private; one instance per active `DetectStream` call with a model loaded. Spawns a daemon thread (`cv-detectstream-reader`) that drains the *rest* of `request_iterator` into a `LatestOnlyMailbox`. `.next()` blocks for the next (latest) frame, `None` at end of stream, re-raises whatever the iterator itself raised. `.stop()` closes the mailbox (best-effort).
- `class TrainingServicer(cv_pb2_grpc.TrainingServicer)` — model-registry control plane + dataset fine-tune (CV-TRAINING Phase 2; `docs/plans/done/CV-TRAINING-PLAN.md` §6). `__init__(*, registry=None, model_dir: Path | None = None, dataset_dir: Path | None = None, train_fn: trainer.TrainFn | None = None, max_upload_bytes: int = DEFAULT_MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES)` — holds the **same** `ModelRegistry` instance the inference path routes against. `model_dir`/`dataset_dir` are resolved lazily (`_resolved_model_dir()`/`_resolved_dataset_dir()`, falling back to `Settings.from_env()` when omitted) at each method that actually needs a concrete directory (`StartTraining`, `_publish_artifact`, `UploadDataset`) — **except** `PromoteModel`'s marker persistence, which keeps its own unresolved `self._model_dir is not None` check: a `None` there means "don't persist a marker" (a test-only affordance), not "use the default directory". `registry` is typed loosely (not `ModelRegistry`) to keep this servicer importable without the `cv` extra.
  - **`ListModels(Empty) -> ModelList`** — one `ModelInfo{id, version="", stage}` per roster id (`sorted`), `stage="active"` for the current default, `"available"` for the rest. Returns an **empty** `ModelList` (never aborts) when `registry is None` (echo mode / no model loaded).
  - **`PromoteModel(ModelRefMsg) -> Ack`** — re-points the shared registry default via `registry.promote(id)` **and** persists the choice to the active-model marker (`cv_service.training.marker`) for restart-survival. `Ack{ok:true}` on success. A bad id is a **normal reported outcome, never an abort**: unknown id, empty id, or `registry is None` all return `Ack{ok:false, message}`.
  - **`StartTraining(TrainingJobSpec) -> stream TrainingProgress`** — resolves the dataset (`trainer.build_spec`, catching `dataset.DatasetNotFoundError` as a terminal reported `FAILED`), then delegates the actual job-lifecycle state machine to `cv_service.training.orchestrator.run_training_job`, translating each plain `JobEvent` it yields into a `cv_pb2.TrainingProgress` (one `RUNNING` per epoch, then exactly one terminal `SUCCEEDED`/`FAILED`). Cancellation is wired via `context.is_active()`/`context.add_callback()`. **Never auto-promotes** — the operator promotes deliberately via `PromoteModel`. Device-agnostic (CUDA if present, else CPU — slow on this Intel box, logged once).
  - **`UploadDataset(stream DatasetChunk) -> UploadAck`** — client-streaming: buffers incoming bytes to a temp file under the datasets root (checking `dataset.is_safe_dataset_id` and the byte cap per-chunk, `context.abort()`ing `INVALID_ARGUMENT`/`RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED` for protocol-level problems), then delegates zip validation/extraction/atomic-replace to `cv_service.training.dataset.land_dataset`, translating its plain `UploadOutcome` into a `cv_pb2.UploadAck`. Content problems (corrupt zip, zip-slip entry, malformed tree, zero chunks) are **reported** (`ok:false`), never aborted. `trainer.py`/`StartTraining` are **not** touched by this RPC.
  - `_publish_artifact(best_weights, spec) -> str` — copies the produced `best.pt` into the model dir under `trainer.output_model_id(...)` and `registry.register()`s it so `ListModels`/`PromoteModel` see it immediately, without a restart. Passed into `orchestrator.run_training_job` as its `publish_artifact` callback.
- **`InferenceServicer.DetectPulled(request_iterator, context)` (MEDIA-SOT-PLAN wave M3)** — the pull counterpart to `DetectStream`; full design in "Pull mode (`DetectPulled`)" below. One-line shape: claim the first `PullControl` (must carry `source_url`, else `INVALID_ARGUMENT`), open a `cv_service.pull.source.PullSource` (`UNAVAILABLE` on failure), start a `_PullControlReader` background thread (mirrors `_StreamReader`, but for the CONTROL stream — overwrite-to-latest, never drop-and-count), drive a `cv_service.pull.loop.PullDecodeLoop`, and for every SERVED frame build a synthetic `cv_pb2.FrameRequest` and hand it to the SAME `_handle_request`/`_echo` this class already has — no second inference path. `PullStalledError` (source stops producing) → `UNAVAILABLE`; a `stream_id` mismatch on a later `PullControl` → `INVALID_ARGUMENT`; `stop=true` or the client half-closing → clean end. New constructor kwarg: **`pull_source_open=`** (injectable `PullSource` factory; omitted → `cv_service.pull.source.open_source`, lazily imported so this class stays importable without the `cv` extra even though `DetectPulled` itself needs it to do anything real).
- Module-level `DetectPulled`-only helpers (none of them touch tracking): `_PullControlState` (thread-safe holder for a call's latest `PullControl`, swapped not mutated), `_PullControlReader` (the background thread above; `.should_stop`/`.error` are its two signals), `_PullStreamIdMismatch`, `_resolve_pull_target_fps`/`_resolve_pull_detect_width` (the `<=0 = deployment default` sentinels, §5.1 fields 7/8), `_downscale_for_detection` (mirrors `DetectionFrameCodec.withDownscaled`'s exact rule: only `>`, not `>=`, aspect preserved, rounded height).

### `cv_service/grpc/server.py`
`def serve(settings: Settings | None = None) -> grpc.Server` — resolves `Settings.from_env()` if none given, builds `grpc.server(futures.ThreadPoolExecutor(max_workers=settings.grpc_workers), options=_KEEPALIVE_SERVER_OPTIONS)`, builds the `ModelRegistry` **once** (`_build_default_registry(settings)`, may be `None`) and one `InferenceGate(settings.max_concurrent_inferences)`, builds and **probes** the `TrackerRegistry` once (`_build_tracker_registry(settings)`, T1 — probing at startup rather than on first frame is the whole point, so the roster an operator sees is the one that actually constructs on this box; a failure here degrades trackers, never blocks startup), passes the same registry to both `InferenceServicer(registry=..., inference_gate=..., settings=..., tracker_registry=...)` and `TrainingServicer(registry=..., model_dir=settings.model_dir, dataset_dir=settings.dataset_dir, max_upload_bytes=settings.max_upload_bytes)` so `PromoteModel` re-points the very registry inference routes against (one source of truth), binds `[::]:{settings.port}`, starts, returns. `def main() -> None` — resolves `Settings` once, calls `serve(settings)`, installs `SIGTERM`/`SIGINT` handlers that call `server.stop(grace=settings.shutdown_grace_seconds)`. `_KEEPALIVE_SERVER_OPTIONS: list[tuple[str, int]]` — module-level constant, HTTP/2 keepalive server options (docs/plans/done/REMOTE-CV-PLAN.md "Transport decisions" P1): `grpc.keepalive_permit_without_calls=1`, `grpc.http2.min_ping_interval_without_data_ms=10000`, `grpc.keepalive_time_ms=30000`, `grpc.keepalive_timeout_ms=10000`.

## Conventions
- Generated stubs live under `cv_service/gen/` and are **never committed** — run `scripts/gen_proto.sh` after cloning and whenever `proto/vision/v1/cv.proto` changes.
- `cv_service/grpc/servicers.py` is the sole translation point between wire types (`cv_pb2.FrameRequest`, `encoding` as an enum int, `cv_pb2.TrackingConfig`/`TargetLock`, `cv_pb2.TrainingProgress`, `cv_pb2.UploadAck`) and every other module's plain types (`width`/`height`/`encoding: str`/`data: bytes`, `Detection`, `TrackingRequest`/`LockRequest`/`FrameOutcome`, `JobEvent`, `UploadOutcome`). `cv_service/inference/detector.py`, `registry.py`, `cv_service/training/dataset.py`, `trainer.py`, `orchestrator.py` and **every module under `cv_service/tracking/`** follow the same rule (none of them import `cv_pb2`). This keeps all of them unit-testable without running codegen first.
- **Proto enum values cross that boundary as their own value-name strings** — `"TRACK_STATE_CONFIRMED"`, `"DETECTOR_REASON_CADENCE"`, `"TRACKING_MODE_FOLLOW"`, `"DETECTION_SOURCE_TRACKER"` — the same convention `detector.py`'s `ENCODING_JPEG`/`ENCODING_BGR24` already used for `ImageEncoding`. The servicer maps them with `cv_pb2.X.Name()`/`.Value()` and keeps **no lookup table**, so there is nothing to drift when the proto grows a value.
- **`cv_service/tracking/` is pure stdlib except `engines/{bytetrack,lk,ncc}.py`.** `engines/base.py` is stdlib too (its `np.ndarray`/`Detection` annotations are `TYPE_CHECKING`-only), which is what lets `track.py`/`lock.py`/`session.py` share its `Box`/`Observation` vocabulary. Two things depend on this: `python -m cv_service.grpc.server` stays runnable with no `cv` extra even though `servicers.py` imports the session at module scope, and the scheduler/lifecycle/lock/session tests run against a fake clock with no frames and no OpenCV. Both are pinned by tests in `tests/tracking/test_registry.py`.
- `cv_service/inference/detector.py` (and, transitively, `cv_service/inference/registry.py`) imports `cv2`/`numpy` at module scope, so importing either at all requires the `cv` extra. `cv_service/grpc/servicers.py`/`server.py` only import them lazily inside their own `_build_default_registry()` functions — so `cv_service.grpc.server`, and hence `python -m cv_service.grpc.server`, stays importable/runnable without the `cv` extra (echo mode).
- `cv_service/config.py` is the one place every `CV_*` env var is read (see its API surface entry above) — no other module calls `os.environ.get` directly.
- Tests live in `tests/` (not under `cv_service/`), mirroring the package layout (`tests/inference/`, `tests/training/`, `tests/grpc/`, `tests/tracking/`, plus `tests/test_config.py`), plain `pytest`, fixtures in `tests/conftest.py` (a tiny synthetic frame, no fixture image files on disk).

## V-d: parallel per-stream inference (`docs/plans/done/MVP2-PLAN.md` §V-d)

**Problem this closes:** pre-V-d, `DetectStream` was `for request in request_iterator: infer(request); yield response` — receiving the next frame off the network only started *after* the current frame's inference finished and its response had been handed back to gRPC, so inference and network transfer never overlapped within one stream; and every stream's `detect()` calls ran fully unbounded/uncoordinated against every other stream's, with nothing protecting the CPU as more streams were added.

**Design chosen — a background reader thread + a 1-slot latest-wins mailbox, per stream, plus a small process-wide semaphore across streams:**

- **Within a stream** (`_StreamReader` + `LatestOnlyMailbox`, both in `cv_service/grpc/servicers.py`/`cv_service/inference/concurrency.py`): `DetectStream` claims the stream's **first** frame directly and synchronously (there's nothing to overlap with before the first frame's own inference even begins), *then* spawns a `_StreamReader` background thread to drain the rest of `request_iterator` into a `LatestOnlyMailbox` while the consumer (the thread gRPC itself drives as the `DetectStream` generator) is inside `detect()`. If a second frame arrives in the mailbox before the consumer has taken the first, it silently replaces it (`.dropped` counts this) — **never a growing backlog**, always the newest available frame. **Response ordering per stream is still always increasing/in-order** (frames are only ever consumed and yielded one at a time, in the order the reader thread received them, minus whichever got overwritten) — this was a deliberate simplicity choice, not a requirement: `adapter-cv-grpc/MODULE.md`'s "Correlation design" section was read first and confirms the Java side matches responses back to pending futures **by their own `sequence` field**, independently per response (`ConcurrentHashMap<Long, CompletableFuture<DetectionResult>>` keyed by sequence, proven under out-of-order server replies by its own `detectCorrelatesInterleavedResponsesBySequence` test) — so mild reordering would have been tolerated too, but keeping this side in-order is simpler to reason about and costs nothing.
  - **Why the first frame is claimed synchronously rather than also going through the reader thread:** confirmed empirically (not assumed) while building this — spawning the reader thread immediately and letting *it* claim frame 0 too creates a real, reproducible race between the reader thread racing ahead through an already-available burst of frames and the consumer's very first `mailbox.get()` call; with a zero-latency in-memory iterator (exactly what this module's own tests use, and plausible for a very bursty real sender too) the reader can legitimately win that race and have already overwritten frame 0 with a later frame before the consumer ever asks for anything. Claiming frame 0 directly removes the possibility entirely, and matches reality: there's no earlier frame to be overlapping the first one's *reception* with anyway.
  - **Why drops are expected to be rare in production, not just "possible in theory":** the mailbox only drops when a *second* new frame arrives while a *first* one is still unconsumed — i.e. only when the sender is at least two frames ahead of what's been inferred+responded-to. `adapter-cv-grpc` (the only real caller) allows at most a small, bounded number of frames in flight per stream at once (see its own MODULE.md "Failure semantics"/correlation design) rather than sending unboundedly far ahead of received responses, so in practice the reader is rarely more than one frame ahead of the consumer. This module's own tests deliberately *do* trigger drops (with instantaneous/fast fake iterators and a gated fake detector) specifically because proving the drop behavior needs to force that "sender got ahead" condition on demand — that's a test-engineering choice, not evidence it's common in production.
- **Across streams** (`InferenceGate`, in `cv_service/inference/concurrency.py`): every `detect()` call, regardless of which stream it's for, acquires the same process-wide `InferenceGate` (default size `min(2, cpu_count // 2)`, floored at 1, overridable via `CV_MAX_CONCURRENT_INFERENCES`) before running and releases it after — bounding how many inferences may run *concurrently* across the whole process, independent of how many streams happen to be open. This is a plain semaphore around each stream's own existing consumer thread, not a new thread pool — inference was already running on a per-stream thread (gRPC's own `ThreadPoolExecutor`, see `serve()`), this only caps concurrent admission into `detect()`.
  - **Why 2, not `cpu_count`, and the honest OpenVINO/torch interplay:** a single Ultralytics/PyTorch `detect()` call is already internally multi-threaded (intra-op parallelism) — verified in this task's own dev environment, `torch.get_num_threads()` reported `6` on a 12-logical-core box, entirely PyTorch's own default heuristic, nothing this module configures or was asked to configure. So even 2 *concurrent* `detect()` calls can already ask for `2 x 6 = 12` threads' worth of work on that same 12-thread box — deliberately in the right ballpark rather than under- or wildly over-subscribing it, and it's capped at 2 regardless of core count because the realistic demo load (1-3 simultaneous streams) never needs more concurrency than that anyway. **What was NOT independently re-verified**: OpenVINO's own CPU-plugin thread auto-configuration (the Docker image's actual inference backend, see CP-a) — OpenVINO isn't installed in this dev environment (it's `pip install`ed only inside the Dockerfile, deliberately kept out of the `cv` extra), so this reasoning is carried over from the verified PyTorch behavior on the assumption OpenVINO's own default thread auto-tuning is comparably core-proportional (documented OpenVINO CPU plugin behavior, not measured here) — a real difference here would only change the *justification's* precision, not the gate's correctness, since `CV_MAX_CONCURRENT_INFERENCES` is an env var precisely so this number can be tuned without a code change once it's run against the real Docker image under load.
- **Sync `grpc.server`, not `grpc.aio`:** the existing synchronous gRPC server (`futures.ThreadPoolExecutor`-backed, see `serve()`) was kept rather than switching to `grpc.aio`. The concurrency this task needed — overlap within a stream, a bound across streams — is fully expressible with plain `threading` primitives (`LatestOnlyMailbox`, `InferenceGate`) layered onto the existing per-call-gets-its-own-thread model; a switch to `grpc.aio` would have meant rewriting `DetectStream` as an `async def`, `detect()`'s blocking `model.predict()` call would need `run_in_executor`-wrapping to avoid blocking the event loop anyway (it's not async-native), and the Dockerfile/test setup would gain a second async runtime to reason about for no behavioral gain this task needed. Kept simple; revisit only if a future task needs `grpc.aio`-specific features (e.g. true async model backends).
- **Echo mode (no model loaded) is untouched**: `DetectStream` special-cases `self._detector is None` to the original plain `for request in request_iterator: yield echo(request)` loop — no reader thread, no gate, nothing to overlap or bound without a model in the loop.
- **Graceful teardown (client cancel):** verified empirically against this grpc version (not assumed) — cancelling a call unblocks a **background** thread's `next(request_iterator)` within milliseconds, the exact same as it would the main thread; `_StreamReader`'s reader thread is therefore reliably unblocked and exits on its own once a stream ends for any reason, and `DetectStream`'s `finally: reader.stop()` (closing the mailbox) handles the rest. Covered by `test_reader_thread_does_not_leak_on_client_cancel` (real `grpc.server` + `grpc.insecure_channel`, not a fake) and `test_reader_thread_does_not_leak_on_normal_stream_completion` (`tests/grpc/test_detect_stream_concurrency.py`).

**Measured/estimated effect on detection rate:**
- *Per-stream overlap (goal 1), deterministic (fake-clock, immune to real CPU-inference noise — controlled `time.sleep`s standing in for network transit and inference)*: when simulated network transit is comparable to or larger than simulated inference (the case this task's goal targets — hiding real gRPC network latency behind compute), overlap measured **1.3-1.7x faster than the naive receive-then-infer bound** with zero frames dropped (`transit=50ms/infer=50ms/n=5`: 301ms measured vs 500ms naive-serial, 1.66x; `transit=100ms/infer=50ms/n=10`: 1102ms vs 1500ms, 1.36x) — the naive-serial bound is exactly what the pre-V-d `for request in iterator: infer(); yield` loop's wall time equals by construction (each frame pays `transit + infer` in strict sequence), so this is a direct, honest A/B, not just a measurement of the new code in isolation.
- *Cross-stream (goal 2), real Ultralytics `.pt` CPU inference at `imgsz=416`, illustrative only*: 1 stream alone sustained **~10-14 detections/sec** and 3 concurrent streams sustained **~9-10 detections/sec in aggregate** (~3/sec each) on this task's dev machine — **caveat, stated honestly**: this box was under real, visible, concurrent load from other agents' own builds/IDE for the whole task (`uptime` load average 4.7-7.9 on a 12-thread box, several `java`/Maven processes and an IDE running at the same time — confirmed via `ps`), so the absolute numbers vary run-to-run by 30%+ and are **not** a clean isolated benchmark; presented for directional insight, not a performance SLA. The qualitative, more load-independent finding is the one that matters for the goal: 3 streams' *aggregate* throughput does not collapse to a fraction of 1 stream's (it stays in the same ballpark), and each stream gets a fair, bounded share (~1/3 each) rather than any one stream starving the others or all three uncoordinatedly thrashing the CPU against each other — which is exactly what `InferenceGate` is for. The gate's own admission-control correctness (distinct from real-inference throughput) is verified deterministically and without any real-model noise by `test_inference_gate_caps_concurrent_detects_across_four_streams` (4 contending fake streams, gate size 2 → observed peak concurrency is exactly 2, never more).

## Pull mode (`DetectPulled`, `docs/plans/active/MEDIA-SOT-PLAN.md` wave M3)

**What changes, and what deliberately does not.** `DetectStream` receives frame BYTES over the RPC; `DetectPulled` receives a declarative `PullControl` (restated on every message, same self-healing doctrine `TrackingConfig` already uses — a one-shot control message can be lost with no error/retry) and dials `source_url` (a mediamtx path, D2) itself. Everything from "one decoded frame" downward is **the exact same code**: a served `PulledFrame` is packaged into a synthetic `cv_pb2.FrameRequest` and handed to `_handle_request` — the same function, same `SessionRegistry`, same `ModelRegistry`/`InferenceGate`, same `_echo` fallback `DetectStream` uses. There is no second inference path to keep in sync; `DetectPulled` only adds a second way frames arrive at the first one.

**Three seams (D7), `cv_service/pull/`:**
- **`source.py` — `PullSource` protocol.** `OpenCvPullSource` (`cv2.VideoCapture(url, cv2.CAP_FFMPEG)`) is the sole backend, M0's measured choice (`docs/conclusions/CV-PULL-SPIKE.md` §2/§7: 2.3-4.1 ms decode p50 on this laptop, 1.9-4.3 ms on the GB4005, both far below PyAV's ~33 ms). `open_source(backend=...)` logs and falls back to `opencv` for any other name (`CV_PULL_DECODER=pyav` is accepted, not implemented — never a crash) — the protocol boundary is what makes a Pi's `v4l2m2m` or the GB4005's VAAPI decoder a LATER SWAP, not a rewrite of `loop.py`. `rtsp_transport` has no per-`VideoCapture`-instance knob in cv2 — the only lever is the process-wide `OPENCV_FFMPEG_CAPTURE_OPTIONS` env var, so `_open_with_rtsp_transport` serializes every `open()` call on a module lock and restores whatever value was there before; this only serializes the brief `open()` window, never the steady-state read loop, so two pulls with different transports never contend once both are running.
- **`clock.py` — `CaptureClock`.** `capturedAt = anchorWallclock + (pts - anchorPts)` (`CV_PULL_CLOCK_MODE=anchor`, M0's choice) or plain receipt wallclock (`arrival`). Re-anchors when `capture_skew_millis` (= arrival − the anchor-derived estimate, i.e. the CHANGE in transit latency since the last anchor, not raw latency — see the module's own docstring for the derivation) exceeds `CV_PULL_CLOCK_REANCHOR_THRESHOLD_MILLIS` (default 100 ms — reuses the plan's own 10-minute drift *gate* number as the per-event trigger, not a value §5.1/§5.5 pins). M0 measured `anchor` at ±15 ms typical / ~80 ms worst spike over 10 minutes against a synthetic source sharing this host's clock — a measured FLOOR, not a ceiling; a real camera's own oscillator was explicitly out of M0's reach (`docs/conclusions/CV-PULL-SPIKE.md` §4).
- **`loop.py` — `PullDecodeLoop`.** A background reader thread calls `source.read()` as fast as the source produces frames, `put()`-ing each into a single-slot, NON-blocking `_LatestOnlyMailbox` (a straight port of `spikes/pull/sampler.py`'s own class — deliberately distinct from `cv_service.inference.concurrency.LatestOnlyMailbox`, whose `get()` BLOCKS: that shape suits `DetectStream`'s reader, which has nothing else to do while waiting; this loop's consumer instead runs `DeadlineSampler.sample_due()` on its own schedule and only wants to PEEK when a deadline is due). `frames()` is the public iterator: one `(PulledFrame, captured_at_millis, PullDiagnostics)` per SERVED deadline, until `should_continue()` turns false (clean end) or the source stalls (`PullStalledError`, `CV_PULL_STALL_TIMEOUT_MILLIS`, watched both by the reader's own "no frame in N ms" check and, redundantly, by `OpenCvPullSource`'s own `CAP_PROP_READ_TIMEOUT_MSEC`). The mailbox itself is purely mechanical (`put()` only reports whether it overwrote an unconsumed frame); `PullDecodeLoop._awaiting_consumer` — true from the moment a served frame is `yield`-ed until `frames()` is resumed for the next one, i.e. exactly the span `servicers.py` spends running inference synchronously — is what decides whether an overwrite is a genuine `dropped_frames` count (see D8 below, docs/conclusions/MEDIA-SOT-RESULTS.md §6).

**`DeadlineSampler` — ported, not re-derived**, from `spikes/pull/sampler.py` (itself a Python port of `StreamPipeline.sampleDue`/`armScheduleAt`, `feat/cv-rate-control`). Two properties are exactly what a careless port loses, and both survived: **one clock read per iteration** (`sample_due(now_ns)` takes the clock reading as a parameter and never calls `time.*` itself — `loop.py`'s own module docstring states this as a structural fact, and `tests/pull/test_sampler.py::test_sample_due_never_reads_the_clock_itself` checks the source for it) and the **`max(now, ...)` debt clamp** (a schedule more than one interval late restarts from `now + interval`, counting the skipped deadlines as `missed_deadlines` rather than firing a catch-up burst). `retarget(target_fps)` is new here (the spike's sampler was fixed at construction) — `PullControl.target_fps` is HOT, so it can change on any message; retargeting only changes the interval used by FUTURE arms, never resets the already-armed next deadline, so a rate-controller update takes effect within one interval with no special-cased reset.

**D8 (MEDIA-SOT-PLAN decision, CLAUDE.md rule 9) — every GENUINE discard is counted, on the wire.** The reader thread's mailbox always overwrites, never queues: a stalled consumer (a slow model, `InferenceGate` contention) never falls behind on FRESHNESS, it only ever loses frames it would have discarded as stale anyway. **`dropped_frames` (field 19) counts only overwrites that land while `_awaiting_consumer` is true** — a served frame superseded while out for inference, or backlog thrown away because the detector was busy — **not** every mailbox overwrite: most overwrites happen while `frames()` is merely polling for its next scheduled deadline (the source simply outran `target_fps`), and that is downsampling working as designed, never counted as a drop (fixed post-measurement, docs/conclusions/MEDIA-SOT-RESULTS.md §6 — M9 found a healthy 30fps-source/10fps-target pull stream reporting `droppedInFlight: 1027` against `submittedFps: 9.987`, almost exactly the 3:1 non-selection ratio, not a real problem). Two paired tests in `tests/pull/test_loop.py` pin the distinction: `test_dropped_frames_stays_zero_when_only_downsampling_happens` (a source faster than target, drained promptly, must report `dropped_frames == 0` throughout) and `test_d8_dropped_frames_climb_while_served_frames_stay_fresh_under_a_stalled_consumer` (a stalled consumer — simulating a slow detector, `spikes/pull/rate_stall_bench.py`'s own scenario, `docs/conclusions/CV-PULL-SPIKE.md` §3: 754 dropped over a 25s/800ms-stall run pre-fix — must still show the count climbing once a served frame is actually out with the caller, while every served frame's own age at consumption stays under 200 ms throughout, i.e. never grows with the backlog). The standing lesson this guards: an UNCOUNTED drop is how `effectiveFps` sat at 7.58 against a configured 10 for a whole release (`docs/conclusions/CV-RATE-BUDGET.md`) — and, per M9, a MISCOUNTED one is just as misleading.

**`InferenceServicer.DetectPulled`** (`grpc/servicers.py`) wires the three seams together, translating each served `PulledFrame` into a `cv_pb2.FrameRequest` (`stream_id`/worker-minted monotonic `sequence`/`timestamp_millis=captured_at_millis`/downscaled BGR24 `data` at `detect_width` or `CV_PULL_MAX_WIDTH`/the latest `PullControl`'s `model_id`/`model_version`/`confidence_threshold`/`tracking`/`camera_pose`), calling `_handle_request(request, session)` unchanged, then decorating the returned `DetectionResponse` with fields 16-21 (`decode_millis` from the frame itself; `source_fps`/`achieved_fps`/`dropped_frames`/`missed_deadlines`/`capture_skew_millis` from `PullDiagnostics`). **Reused verbatim, not re-implemented**: `SessionRegistry`/`StreamTrackingSession` (same pool, keyed the same way — a reconnecting PULL resumes its book/gallery/lock exactly like a reconnecting PUSH stream does), `InferenceGate`, `ModelRegistry` (composite `model_id` keeps working — nothing about `_detect_via_registry` knows which transport called it), `_tracked_response`/`_echo`. The "never crash-loop for lack of a model" guard is re-checked explicitly at the top of `DetectPulled` (`no_model = self._detector is None and self._registry is None`) because `_handle_request`'s own detector branch assumes one of the two is set — `DetectStream` gets this for free from an early return that skips session/reader setup entirely; `DetectPulled` still wants decode/rate diagnostics even with no model loaded, so it takes the cheaper route of branching only the final `_echo`-vs-`_handle_request` call.

**The two-background-thread shape, and how it differs from `DetectStream`'s one.** `_PullControlReader` mirrors `_StreamReader`'s role (drains the *rest* of the request stream in the background) but for a fundamentally different kind of message: a `PullControl` is OVERWRITTEN into `_PullControlState`, never dropped-and-counted the way a video frame is — there is no D8-style loss concept for control state, because a superseded message's fields are exactly as valid as a newer one's the instant it lands. `.should_stop` becomes true for ANY reason the control stream ends (`stop=true`, the client half-closing, or a read error) — `DetectPulled`'s main loop has one signal to check regardless of why; `.error` (set only for a real problem — currently just `_PullStreamIdMismatch`) is what turns a clean end into an `INVALID_ARGUMENT` abort after the frame loop itself has already finished. Structurally: `PullDecodeLoop`'s OWN reader thread pulls video frames; `_PullControlReader` pulls control messages; `DetectPulled`'s generator body (the thread gRPC drives) is the third, consuming `PullDecodeLoop.frames(should_continue=...)` and calling into `_handle_request` — three threads per call where `DetectStream` has two (its own generator body + `_StreamReader`).

**Call semantics implemented exactly per §5.1**, verified in `tests/pull/test_detect_pulled.py`: missing `source_url` on the first message → `INVALID_ARGUMENT`; an unopenable or later-stalled source → `UNAVAILABLE`; a `stream_id` mismatch on any later `PullControl` → `INVALID_ARGUMENT`; `stop=true` and client half-close both drain cleanly (no abort); `sequence` is worker-minted, monotonic, starting at 1, no correlation map; a mid-call `model_id` swap takes effect on the next served frame with no special-case code (the registry resolves fresh every call); `detect_width` downscales exactly like `DetectionFrameCodec.withDownscaled` (only `>`, aspect preserved, rounded height) and a narrower frame passes through untouched.

**The one comment that inverted under this wave**, fixed where it was found (`_handle_request`'s `session.process(now_millis=time.monotonic() * 1000.0, ...)` call): the old comment distrusted `request.timestamp_millis` as "another machine's clock" — true under `DetectStream` (the JVM stamps it), no longer literally true under `DetectPulled` (this SAME process's `CaptureClock` stamps it). The code is unchanged either way — the duty cycle is genuinely about wall time THIS HOST has spent since its last detector pass, not about when a frame was captured, and a monotonic clock is immune to the NTP corrections a capture timestamp is not — but the comment now says why that holds under both transports instead of asserting a premise (remote clock) that only one of them has.

## Model registry & composite mode (CP-b, `docs/plans/done/CV-MODELS-PLAN.md`)

**Problem this closes:** before CP-b, `DetectStream` hard-loaded exactly one `YoloDetector` at construction time; any other `request.model_id` was logged once and silently served the same default — there was no way for a client to actually pick between the two local checkpoints this repo ships (`yolo11n.pt`, a general COCO model, and `orion12l.pt`, a user-provided 53MB YOLO12-L military-vehicle model), let alone run both on the same frame.

**Design chosen — a lazy `{model_id -> YoloDetector}` registry, keyed by what's actually on disk, plus a comma-separated composite mode:**

- **Roster discovery** (`registry.discover_roster`, called once by `build_default_registry()` at servicer construction): scans `cv-service/` (`Settings.model_dir`'s default — the same directory `YOLO("x.pt")`'s own cwd-relative download/cache behavior already uses — see "`CV_MODEL` / weights location" below, and where the Dockerfile's `WORKDIR` puts things too) for every `*.pt` file and `*_openvino_model` export directory, plus always includes the resolved default (`settings.model`) even if discovery didn't find it there. On this repo's checkout that roster is `{"yolo11n.pt": ..., "yolo26n.pt": ..., "orion12l.pt": ..., "yoloe-26s-seg-pf.pt": ...}` (or, inside the Docker image, with `yolo11n_openvino_model` added — see the Docker gotcha below) — **logged once at INFO at startup** (`"cv-service model registry roster: [...] (default=...)"`), so an operator can see exactly which ids are routable without reading code.
- **Lazy loading**: no model is constructed (no ultralytics weights load, no warmup `predict()`) until first requested. Each is cached for the process's lifetime after that.
- **Routing** (`ModelRegistry.resolve`): `request.model_id` may be one id or a comma-separated list. Every requested id not in the roster is dropped with a once-per-distinct-id INFO log (same log-and-serve-default contract as before CP-b — old clients that never set `model_id`, or clients naming a genuinely unknown model, see zero behavior change). If *nothing* in the request resolved (empty, all-unknown, or a roster id that itself failed to load), falls back to the default model. A **partially**-unknown composite list (e.g. `"orion12l.pt,typo"`) keeps its known member(s) rather than discarding the whole request, since at least one requested model *was* real.
- **Composite mode** (`registry.detect_composite`): every resolved model runs `detect()` on the *same* frame, gated individually through the existing process-wide `InferenceGate` (no separate bound for composite), and detections are concatenated. `inference_millis` returned is the **sum** of every member's own `inference_millis` — composite work is strictly more compute run sequentially on one stream's consumer thread, never overlapped (V-d's overlap is about *network receipt* vs. *inference*, not about running two models at once). Confidence filtering already happens per-model inside each `YoloDetector.detect()` call; composite mode adds no second filtering pass.

**Composite mode: no free proto field, so labels get prefixed instead.** `Detection` (`proto/vision/v1/cv.proto`) has no spare field to carry which model produced a detection (`label`/`confidence`/`box` are the only three fields); adding one is a wire-format change out of scope for this module. Instead, when more than one model actually ran, every detection's `label` gets prefixed `"{short_name}:{label}"` (e.g. `"orion12l:tank"`, `"yolo11n:person"`) — verified safe: `adapter-overlay`'s `Java2DOverlayRenderer` treats `Detection.label()` as a fully opaque string (only ever hashed to pick a stable box color and drawn as literal text). A useful side effect: because the hash includes the prefix, `"yolo11n:person"` and a hypothetical `"orion12l:person"` get *different* colors automatically. **Single-model requests are completely unaffected**.

**Java plumb verification (read-only — vision-application/adapter-cv-grpc/vision-proto were NOT modified by this task):**
`PipelineConfig.model` (a `ModelRef(id, version)`, `vision-domain/src/main/java/com/drones/vision/domain/model/PipelineConfig.java:43`) reaches the wire intact:
- `adapters/adapter-cv-grpc/src/main/java/com/drones/vision/adapter/cvgrpc/GrpcDetectionPort.java:263` — `.setModelId(config.model().id())` (and `:264` `.setModelVersion(config.model().version())`) inside `buildRequest()`, building the `FrameRequest` sent to this service.
- `proto/vision/v1/cv.proto:30` — `FrameRequest.model_id` (`string model_id = 8;`) is exactly the field `ModelRegistry.resolve` reads (`request.model_id`).
- **Verdict: the plumb is intact, zero Java changes needed for registry/composite routing to work today.** The only gap is on the *default value* side, not the plumbing: `PipelineConfig.defaults()` (`PipelineConfig.java:107`) ships `new ModelRef("yolo", "latest")` — `"yolo"` isn't (and was never) a roster id either before or after this task, so it already hit the pre-existing "unknown id, log once, serve default" path and continues to after CP-b unchanged. Wiring an actual model picker into `PipelineConfig.model` is a UI/settings-surface task, out of this module's scope.

**Measured per-model latency and memory (this dev machine, 12 logical cores, `torch.get_num_threads()`=6, `imgsz=416`, plain PyTorch `.pt` weights — not the Docker image's OpenVINO IR; numbers are directional not a clean isolated benchmark):**

| model | construct+warmup | detect() mean (20 calls, 64x48 synthetic frame) | detect() min/max | RSS after load |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `yolo11n.pt` (general, 5.6MB) | ~4.0s | **58.5ms** | 46 / 96ms | ~416MB (+363MB from Python/torch baseline) |
| `orion12l.pt` (military YOLO12-L, 53MB) | ~0.9s (torch/cv2 already warm from loading yolo11n first) | **343ms** | 281 / 451ms | ~608MB (+184MB more, both models resident) |
| composite (`yolo11n.pt,orion12l.pt`) | n/a | **~346ms** (sum of both members' own `inference_millis`) | 303 / 392ms | (both already resident above) |

`orion12l` is confirmed **~5-6x slower per frame** than `yolo11n` on CPU — expected for a YOLO12-**L**arge vs. a YOLO11-**n**ano checkpoint, and directly explains why composite mode's cost is dominated by whichever member is the heavier one, not split evenly. Both models loaded simultaneously (the composite/registry-with-both-cached scenario) cost **~608MB RSS** total on this process — plan for at least that much headroom (plus per-stream buffers) if composite mode is enabled by default rather than opt-in.

## Tracking engine (T1, `docs/plans/done/TRACKING-PLAN.md` + `docs/extracts/TRACKING-ORCHESTRATION.md`)

**Problem this closes:** `DetectStream` ran one full detector pass per received frame, and detections carried no identity across frames — a box was a fresh anonymous rectangle every time, so nothing downstream could say "that is the same car". Two consequences: the CPU cost of watching one target was the full detector cost, forever; and `trackId` did not exist for the UI, for geolocation trails, or for click-to-follow.

**Design chosen — two perception loops on the same stream, running at very different rates:**

| Mode | Every frame | Detector pass when |
|---|---|---|
| `OFF` (default) | — | every received frame. **Byte-identical to pre-T1 behavior** |
| `ASSOCIATE` | associate the detector's boxes to existing tracks | every received frame — cv-service only ever receives *sampled* frames, so there is nothing left to duty-cycle |
| `FOLLOW` | `engine.update(frame)` on the locked target | any of: cadence elapsed · tracker reported lost · no lock held · predicted box collapsed/left frame · coasted past `max_age_frames` |

### Measured on this box (12 logical cores, load avg ~0.7, `opencv-python 5.0.0.93`, `ultralytics 8.4.104`, 640×360 frames)

Directional, not a clean isolated benchmark — same caveat every other number in this file carries.

| What | mean | p95 |
|---|---|---|
| `lk` `update()` — 40 corners, incl. full-frame BGR→grey | **0.525 ms** | 0.619 ms |
| `ncc` `update()` — 80×60 template in a 2× window | **0.243 ms** | 0.283 ms |
| `bytetrack` `associate()` — 10 detections | **0.780 ms** | 0.857 ms |
| **session per tracker-only frame, end to end** (`lk`, incl. JPEG decode) | **2.40 ms** | 2.79 ms |
| **session per tracker-only frame, end to end** (`ncc`, incl. JPEG decode) | **1.83 ms** | 1.94 ms |
| — of which `decode_frame`, 154 KB pure-noise JPEG (worst case) | 2.14 ms | 3.18 ms |
| — of which `decode_frame`, 27 KB realistic-content JPEG | 0.61 ms | 0.82 ms |
| `yolo26n.pt` `detect()` on the same 640×360 JPEG, `imgsz=416`, for contrast | **22.8 ms** | 23.7 ms |

**The two numbers that matter.** The engine itself costs **0.24–0.53 ms/frame**, i.e. **43×–95× cheaper** than a `yolo26n` pass on the same frame (and 650×–1400× cheaper than an `orion12l` pass at ~343 ms). End to end through the session the tracker-only frame costs **1.8–2.4 ms on a pathological all-noise JPEG**, of which the **frame decode is the dominant term, not the tracker** — on realistic content the same path is ~0.9–1.1 ms. Recorded explicitly because it says where the next optimization is if one is ever needed (a BGR24 feed or a smaller frame, not a faster tracker).

**`FOLLOW` duty ratio, measured over 300 frames at 15 fps with the default 2000 ms cadence: 10 detector passes / 300 frames = exactly 1 in 30 (3.3%), i.e. 0.50 detector passes per second instead of 15.** The detector pass rate drops **30×**; the mean per-frame server cost drops from ~23.6 ms (`ASSOCIATE`: a detector pass plus an association every frame) to ~3.2 ms (`FOLLOW`: one pass in thirty plus a tracker update), a **~7×** reduction per stream. Both figures come from `tests/grpc/test_detect_stream_tracking.py`'s fake-clock harness plus the measured per-call costs above. The end-to-end before/after is wave T8's, below — run against real photographic content and a real detector, but **not against a camera**, for the reason T8's section states.

### Wave T8 — the measured outcome (TRACKING-PLAN §10), and exactly what it is not

**The boundary first, because it determines how every number below should be read. This repo has no camera — hardware tier H1 is unbought (docs/main/TWO-TARGETS-PLAN.md). §10 asks for a camera pointed at a street; that demo was not run, and nothing here is camera footage.**

What *was* run, on this dev box (12 logical cores, under normal IDE load — directional, same caveat as every other number in this file): a 150-frame, 640×360, 15 fps sequence built by **panning a 960×540 crop window across one real 1280×720 photograph** (`datasets/…/bb30d92a-….jpg` — a real truck and real people, real texture, real lighting), JPEG-encoded at ~21 KB/frame, with an opaque bar drawn across frames **70–89** standing in for an occluder. Real objects therefore genuinely traverse the frame and are genuinely hidden for 1.33 s. Everything below the frame source is production code: the real `InferenceServicer.DetectStream`, the real `ModelRegistry`/`yolo26n.pt` detector, the real `TrackerRegistry`/`bytetrack`/`lk` engines, over the frozen wire contract. Frames are paced at **15 fps of real time** deliberately: the `FOLLOW` cadence is wall-clock, so running flat out would have flattered the duty ratio.

| | detector passes | CPU (`process_time`) per 9.96 s of video | tracker-only frames | frames fed → responses |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **`OFF`** (pre-tracking behavior) | 150 = **15.07/s** | 28.92 s = **290% of one core** | — | 150 → 150, **0 dropped** |
| **`ASSOCIATE` / `bytetrack`** (the T8 default) | 150 = **15.07/s** | 29.56 s = **297% of one core** | — | 150 → 150, **0 dropped** |
| **`FOLLOW` / `lk`, verify 2000 ms** | 5 = **0.50/s** | 1.29 s = **13% of one core** | 145, `tracker_millis` mean **1.81 ms**, p95 2.00 | 150 → 150, **0 dropped** |

Repeat run: `OFF` 30.25 s, `ASSOCIATE` 29.17 s, `FOLLOW` 1.31 s — so run-to-run spread is ~±4%.

**The two numbers that matter, measured rather than claimed:**

1. **Turning tracking on by default costs nothing measurable.** `ASSOCIATE` vs `OFF` is 29.56 s vs 28.92 s of CPU on run 1 and 29.17 s vs 30.25 s on run 2 — i.e. the association pass sits *inside* the run-to-run noise of the detector-only baseline. That is the number that justifies wave T8 flipping the default at all, and it is why the flip did not need a feature flag to hide behind.
2. **§10's "the CPU drop" — detector passes 15.07/s → 0.50/s (30.1×), CPU 297% → 13% of one core (22.9×).** Read off the response's own `detector_ran`/`tracker_millis` fields, not `htop` — which is what TRACKING-ORCHESTRATION §7 asked for, and it works identically in every placement including one that is flying.

**Track-id stability, and the occluder** (`ASSOCIATE`, ids as cv-service allocated them):

| id | label | frames | behavior |
|---|---|---|---|
| `#1` | person | 0–149, **150 of 150** | one id for the entire sequence, no gap |
| `#2` | person | 3–149, 105 frames | **gap exactly 70–89 — the occluder window — then returns as `#2`** |
| `#3` | truck | 49–149, 101 frames | enters mid-sequence, one id to the end, no gap |
| `#4` | truck | 149 only, 1 frame | a single-frame id on the last frame — the flicker `min_hits` exists to keep out of the UI |

**`#2` disappearing for precisely the 20 occluded frames and coming back with the same id is the "same box number through a pole" outcome of §10 item 1** — demonstrated, but on a *drawn* occluder over a *panned still*, which is not the same evidence as a car passing a real pole. In `FOLLOW` the locked target held `#1` across all 150 frames including the occlusion, on 5 detector passes.

**What remains unproven until H1 hardware exists** — none of it blocked on code:

- **§10 item 1 on real footage.** Motion blur, rolling shutter, exposure changes, genuine 3-D occlusion and real inter-frame jitter are all absent from a panned still. Association across *those* is the actual claim, and it is untested.
- **§10 item 2's operator-facing half.** The CPU drop is measured above, but "click a box in the Fly cockpit → it locks → watch the flow strip" was not performed: it needs a browser, a live stream and a camera. The wire and API tiers that feed that strip are covered by tests; the demo is not.
- **§10 item 3 (a trail behind a tracked car in the player)** — wave T7 built it against the frozen contract; it has not been seen over camera footage.
- **Every Pi/onboard number**, unchanged from T1: designed-for budgets, not measurements.

The measurement harness was a throwaway script, deliberately not added to `tests/` — it needs real weights, a gitignored dataset image, and ~30 s of wall clock, none of which belong in a suite that must stay green on a clean checkout.

### Engine roster (§5.B) — dictated by what is actually installed

| `engine_id` | Protocol | Mechanism | Assets |
|---|---|---|---|
| **`bytetrack`** | `Associator` (ASSOCIATE default) | ultralytics `BYTETracker` — IoU + Kalman, two-stage high/low-confidence association | none (already a dependency; needs `lap`, now pinned) |
| **`cost`** | `Associator` | `assign.CostAssociator` — the platform's own gated cost matrix + Hungarian, cv-service's own tracks as candidates | none — pure stdlib |
| **`lk`** | `SingleObjectTracker` (FOLLOW default) | `cv2.calcOpticalFlowPyrLK` over ≤40 `goodFeaturesToTrack` corners; median translation + median radial scale | none — core OpenCV `video`, build-info confirmed present |
| **`ncc`** | `SingleObjectTracker` | `cv2.matchTemplate(TM_CCOEFF_NORMED)` in a 2× search window | none — core OpenCV |
| **`flow`** | `MotionCompensator` (motion default) | sparse `cv2.calcOpticalFlowPyrLK` across the WHOLE frame + `cv2.estimateAffinePartial2D(RANSAC)` global fit | none — core OpenCV, same modules `lk` already needs |
| **`pose`** | `MotionCompensator` | camera-attitude trigonometry (yaw/pitch → translation, roll → rotation, both first-order) | none — pure `math`, no assets, no `cv` extra needed |
| **`histogram`** | `AppearanceExtractor` (appearance default) | coarse joint H×S colour histogram, Hellinger metric | none — core OpenCV `imgproc` |

`lk` and `ncc` fail in *different* places, which is why both ship: `lk` needs corners and refuses a feature-poor target (returns `False` from `init`, never raises), while `ncc` needs only that the patch keep looking like itself but drifts under rotation/scale, which `lk` handles. **KCF/CSRT/MOSSE are not options here** — OpenCV 5 removed them and `cv2.legacy` is gone; the only `Tracker*` symbols present are `TrackerMIL` (24 ms/frame, ~50× the budget, rejected) and `TrackerNano`/`TrackerVit`/`TrackerDaSiamRPN`, each of which raises on construction because opencv-python ships no ONNX weights for them. Those three are a deferred, documented seam: a file drop (the Dockerfile carries a commented, ready-to-uncomment `curl`) plus a registry entry, not a redesign.

`flow` and `pose` fail in different places too (wave C2): `flow` needs texture and enough RANSAC agreement — a blank wall, or a moving subject filling the frame, both degrade to `IDENTITY` rather than a guess; `pose` needs an `hfov_degrees > 0` on the wire, which no Java client sends yet (TRACKING-V2-PLAN §2.1's one-liner is a separately-approved follow-up), so it is constructible today but reports itself unavailable on every real stream — `StreamTrackingSession` falls back to `flow` in that case, logged once (see "Ego-motion compensation" below). `flow` is the deployment default (`CV_TRACK_MOTION_ENGINE=flow`) precisely because it needs nothing from Java to start working.

### Six decisions worth knowing before you change this

1. **`TrackerRegistry` hands out factories; `ModelRegistry` caches singletons.** A YOLO model is stateless across streams and expensive to load, so it is cached per id. A tracker is the exact reverse — cheap to build, inherently stateful — so **every call constructs a new engine, and engines are never shared between streams**. Getting this backwards corrupts every stream at once, so it has its own test.
2. **Three protocols, not one** (wave C2 adds `MotionCompensator` to the T1 pair). `bytetrack` can never implement `update(frame)` and `lk` can never implement `associate(detections)`; one protocol would force every engine to stub half of itself on the seam the whole extensibility story rests on. An engine that legitimately served more than one would implement more than one — none of the five shipped ones does.
3. **The tracker path never acquires `InferenceGate`** — the gate bounds concurrent *YOLO* passes, and a 0.5 ms tracker update queueing behind a 343 ms `orion12l` pass on another stream would destroy the design. This is structural, not a convention: `session.py` holds no gate reference and can only reach the detector through the `detect` callable the servicer passes in, which is `_run_detector`, the single acquisition site. Tested with a recording fake gate asserting **zero** acquisitions across 59 consecutive tracker-only frames.
4. **The config is restated on every frame and resolved on change.** `TrackingConfig` rides every `FrameRequest` deliberately (`LatestOnlyMailbox` can silently drop a frame, so a one-shot control message could vanish with no error and no retry; a restated desired state is self-healing). The per-frame cost of that is exactly one protobuf `==` in `_sync_tracking`; `params.resolve()` runs only on a real change. A `TargetLock` applies only on a **strictly greater** `lock_seq`, so restating the same lock 15×/second is a no-op.
5. **`session.py` is ~300 code lines, not the "~80" TRACKING-ORCHESTRATION §2.1 budgeted.** The charter held — no scheduling policy, lifecycle rule or geometry lives there, and the two blocks that did (target selection, the degradation ladder's parameter copy) moved to `lock.py` and `params.py`. What the estimate missed is that FOLLOW alone has five distinct per-frame outcomes (re-anchor, coast, tracker-lost, box-invalid, lost-past-max-age), each sequencing engine + book + lock, on top of the degradation ladder and the engine lifecycle. Recorded rather than quietly split across a ninth file, which would move the seam without moving a responsibility.
6. **In `FOLLOW`, `CONFIRMED` and `COASTING` alternate, and that is the visible signature of the duty cycle.** A verify pass that re-anchors makes the box `CONFIRMED` with `source = DETECTOR`; every frame in between is `COASTING` with `source = TRACKER` — literally "tracker-predicted; the detector has not re-confirmed it on the most recent pass". A locked target is `CONFIRMED` immediately on acquisition rather than serving `min_hits` verify passes (six seconds at the default cadence) first: `min_hits` is an anti-flicker gate against *false positives*, and an operator clicking a box is not one. Carried as `Observation.authoritative`, which engines never set.

### Ego-motion compensation (TRACKING-V2-PLAN wave C2, `docs/conclusions/TRACKING-REVIEW.md` §4.5)

**The defect this closes (REVIEW A1/A2).** Association is IoU-based in image space; when the CAMERA moves, every box in frame moves with it, and nothing told the tracker that — the motion is charged to the objects as if they had moved, and a target that shifts further than its own width between two sampled frames has zero overlap with itself. `flow_gmc.py`/`pose_gmc.py` measure that shift as a `Transform` (`engines/base.py`) and warp a track's predicted box by it BEFORE any IoU is computed against the current frame.

**Scope, deliberately narrower than the port (`engines/base.py`) suggests: FOLLOW only, not ASSOCIATE, in this wave.** ASSOCIATE's association state lives entirely inside ultralytics' `BYTETracker` — its own Kalman filters, its own matching — and this ultralytics version has **no `STrack.multi_gmc`** (verified against the installed version, not assumed). Warping that state would mean reaching into a third-party library's internals one wave before C3 replaces the whole association core with `assign.py`'s `CostAssociator`, which cv-service owns and where the same change is two lines instead of a library patch. So:
- **FOLLOW is compensated**: `session.py`'s `process()` calls `_estimate_motion` once per FOLLOW frame, before the mode branches, and then calls `TrackBook.warp(transform)` — see "Warping the STATE, not the read" below for why that second step, not `predict()` taking a `transform` argument, is the actual mechanism.
